# Investigation Ledger

Records every investigation dispatched under the tiering in `AGENTS.md` 1.3, so the
two-tier split is **measured rather than assumed**. The split was adopted as an
architecture to test, not a settled conclusion; if the mid-tier proves unable to produce
packets good enough to resolve most investigations, this ledger is the evidence that
justifies reverting to Opus as the default investigator.

Record one row per investigation, at the time it completes. Token counts come from the
subagent usage reported to the Orchestrator.

## What the numbers are for

| Metric | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| Escalation rate | If it approaches 1, the mid-tier is not doing its job and the split is only adding a hop. |
| Escalations that materially changed the conclusion | The core value question for the Opus tier. Cheap confirmations are fine; *only* confirmations means the bar is too low. |
| Escalations that merely confirmed | Not waste at low rates — it is what a correctly-calibrated bar produces. Waste if it dominates. |
| Cost per investigation, by tier | The economic claim being tested. |
| Failures traceable to bad investigation | The correctness guardrail. Any occurrence is serious and should be written up in full, not just tallied. |

**Honesty rule.** Record outcomes including the ones that argue against the current
architecture. A ledger kept to justify a decision already made is worthless.

## Log

**Pilot (MC-091).** For the next 5–10 comparable items, each item's rows also record, where
available: elapsed time, token categories, dispatch count, review rounds (and their outcome
tokens), full-suite executions, substantive findings, and later reopenings or escaped defects.
Compare only items of similar risk and complexity; no savings target, and no claim of proven safety
from a small sample. That window has passed: 17 items with their own commit have been
recorded since row 217, which wrote MC-091 up as Report 29 (CHORE-33 28C, CHORE-39, the removal stage,
the upstream sync, CHORE-42, W1a, W1b, the composer stage's S0, S1 and S2, the upstream batch, W2a, W2b-core,
W2b-previews, Escape on alerts stages 1 and 2, and W2c-a). Whether the pilot stands is the
maintainer's call; this note does not evaluate it.

| 4 | 2026-09-21 | Stage B plan gate — falsify the module draft-copy plan (`Agents/Reports/10-stage-b-module-draft-copy-plan.md`) | `opus-reviewer` (Opus 5) | ~128.3k, 51 tool uses | n/a (gate, not investigation) | Verdict: **REJECT**, 11 findings, correctly rejected. Two REJECT-grade findings, both Orchestrator-verified: **R1** the plan's flush-on-unmount claim was false — Tauri desktop has no unload hook, and cold storage reloads bypass the un-cancellable path. **R3** duplicate module ids are reachable (`.risum` imports keep their id), so a bare `findIndex` can clobber the wrong module under commit-on-destroy. Also caught a false perf attribution the Orchestrator had written (R2: post-Stage-A cost is 0.03 ms, not 29.21 ms — the plan summed pre-Stage-A numbers), a test seam that would re-run Stage A's effect (R5), and a broken empty-draft predicate. **The reviewer was itself wrong twice**, both caught on verification: an off-by-one citation (R4) and a replacement count that didn't reproduce (R7: cite the command, not the number). Net: the gate paid for itself several times over. |

| 5 | 2026-09-21 | Does any awaitable "pending DB writes flushed" signal exist, for a Tauri `onCloseRequested` draft flush? | `investigator` (Sonnet 5) | ~58.6k, 27 tool uses | No | **Refuted the feasibility premise** behind the maintainer's chosen option: no awaitable 'writes flushed' signal exists — `dirtySinceLastSave`/`changeTracker` are function-local inside `saveDb()`, and the only exported signal (`saving`) is racy on its true→false edge. No existing teardown path waits on `saveDb()`; reload sites either accept loss or bypass the loop. Flagged unprompted that the migration-lock pattern looks copyable but only wraps the raw write, not the encode/debounce. **The Orchestrator then found the reframing fact** from the investigator's own trail: the save debounce is 500ms trailing and resets on every keystroke, so continuous typing produces zero disk writes today until a pause — today's durability is not per-keystroke, which made the planned Tauri hook and save-loop surgery likely unnecessary. No escalation needed. |

| 6 | 2026-09-21 | Stage B plan gate round 2 — falsify revision 2, including the new save-loop seam (§3.7.4) | `opus-reviewer` (Opus 5, fresh context, explicitly unbound from round 1) | ~130.0k, 44 tool uses | n/a (gate) | Verdict: **REJECT**, 11 findings, 6 REJECT-grade — sharper than round 1. Best finding: revision 2's duplicate-id fix was circular (it validated a stored index using the same bare `findIndex` that produced the wrong element, while *increasing* reachability by firing on debounce/unmount/pagehide/close). Second: reading the Tauri **runtime** (not just typings) showed `onCloseRequested` calls `destroy()` unless `preventDefault()` fires first — revision 2's shape opts out of its own close on any throw, stranding an unclosable window. Third: the flush-correlation proof cited the wrong line (a dirty-block flag, not the data) and relied on an unstated ordering invariant. Also caught a citation off by 2x (3 sites claimed, 6 actual), a fabricated supporting sentence, and a retracted claim still live in the plan. Confirmed sound: the corrected debounce baseline, the resolve-edge choice, and the byte-identity argument. **Orchestrator note: across both Stage B gates, most of the 22 findings were errors in the Orchestrator's own plan text, including two that would have shipped a silent-data-loss path while claiming to close one.** Plan text is not trustworthy on first draft; the gate paid for itself many times over. |

| 7 | 2026-09-21 | Benchmark the partition branch point: per-module vs whole-array snapshot, asset-heavy shape, module-count scaling, `$effect` overhead | `perf-analyzer` (Sonnet 5), two dispatches | ~91.0k + ~157.3k, 57 tool uses | No | **Measurement campaign that replaced three rejected plan revisions with numbers**, reproduced independently by the Orchestrator on every headline figure. Partitioning takes a keystroke from ~29.8 ms (whole-array snapshot) to ~4.2 ms (largest module) / ~0.2 ms (median). Per-element sum equals the whole-array walk (1.01x — shape changes are not a regression). `$effect` scheduling/teardown overhead of N children vs 1 is below measurement noise (0.92-1.01x at N=52/104). Flagged its own unmeasured gap unprompted (effect overhead), then commissioned. **Most valuable result:** snapshot cost tracks node count, not bytes — a 1.03 MB asset-shaped module costs 35.6 ms vs 4.2 ms for an equal-size cjs-heavy one, ~8x at equal size, which bounded the partition's claim to non-asset modules instead of overstating it. |

| 8 | 2026-09-21 | Real-app end-to-end: is the cost the effect or the DOM, and what does it cost at real module counts? | Orchestrator direct (browser pane, live dev server) | in-session | n/a | Live-app measurement (module editor open, real `input` events; all mutations restored and verified): effect 11.5 ms vs DOM ~2.2 ms at 52 modules — the effect is ~84% of the cost, answering the ceiling question and clearing the last planning blocker. 104 modules: 25.1 ms (150% of frame budget), 1.9x over 52 — linearity confirmed on live data. Corrected the harness README's caveat: Chromium is ~2.6x faster than the Node harness on identical data (ratios still valid, absolute numbers don't transfer). Downstream effect work beyond the raw snapshot is ~0.2 ms, negligible. |

| 18 | 2026-09-21 | Planning facts for avatar stages A (stop re-lookups) and C (lazy-mount): call sites, Svelte 5.55.1 equality and each-block semantics, sidebar mechanics, reusable lazy infrastructure, hazards | `investigator` (Sonnet 5), 1 `code-searcher` survey | ~131.8k, 42 tool uses | No | **Useful, with one inference disputed and left for the harness to settle** (later settled empirically by the AV-1 gate, row 20 — the Orchestrator's dispute was correct). The Sidebar's avatar-rebuild effect only skips no-op passes (a lodash `isEqual` guard), so any single rename or avatar change re-resolves every sidebar avatar — the same defect as the character-list `formatChars`, one level up. `LazyPortal.svelte` has zero call sites and is one-shot, so it defers cost without bounding it. List/trash rows and open folders have non-uniform heights, favouring per-item IntersectionObserver over fixed-height windowing. Orchestrator verified the equality/each-block semantics and the zero-`LazyPortal`-users claim. |

| 19 | 2026-09-21 | Planning facts for avatar stages B (plain-HTTP encode) and D (thumbnails): `getFileSrc` callers' dependence on `data:` URLs, cache semantics, resize precedent, invisible thumbnail storage, avatar identity, Tauri cost | `investigator` (Sonnet 5), 1 `code-searcher` survey | ~117.1k, 57 tool uses | No | **Mostly correct, one wrong claim caught at verification.** Findings: the clipboard 'copy as HTML' allow-lists exclude `blob:` (would silently drop images if adopted); revoking `blob:` URLs on LRU eviction is unsafe while `<img>` elements still hold the resolved string; a canvas-thumbnail precedent already exists in production (`BotSettings.svelte`); avatar paths are content-hashed, so a path-keyed thumbnail can't go stale; Tauri uses the stock asset protocol with no custom handler, so full-size decode per icon is architecturally confirmed (cost unmeasured); a pre-existing MIME anomaly labels all assets `image/png`. **Wrong claim:** 'oder.img is never read back anywhere' — `backuplocal.ts` reads it as an asset-map key, and it holds the `getFileSrc` result in `db.characterOrder`, so on plain HTTP each folder image is persisted as a full base64 `data:` URL in the save; AV-3+ must keep `blob:` URLs out of that field. Orchestrator verified the corrected claim and the other citations. |

Corrected its own dispatched `code-searcher` unprompted (the copy button parses a detached document, not live DOM). **Defect:** it claimed streaming writes coalesce to at most one per animation frame while its own next paragraph described a per-chunk remount under the default `'off'` — the two cannot both hold. | 102 | 2026-09-23 | Containment experiment: does `content-visibility` pay for itself, and what is a chat-to-chat switch worth? | Orchestrator, in the browser pane (dev server started by the maintainer; a temporary character with two 3000-message chats, removed afterwards through `removeChar` and verified across a reload) | n/a | Yes (live) | **Measured, and the answer is no** (numbers: Report 19 §8). Also found that the intrinsic-size estimate is load-bearing: a guessed 220 px collapsed the scroll height by 25.3%, while the measured 297 px average held it to 0.2%. Scroll-up anchoring and off-screen seek both passed unchanged; the screenshot and WebKit checks were not run and are stated as such. **Separately worth more than the rule:** a chat-to-chat switch at 600 mounted costs 95.6 ms and is almost all script, of which the `ReloadGUIPointer` bump in `changeChatTo` is ~31% — a cheap deferral. **Method limits stated rather than papered over:** the DevTools Performance panel is unreachable from the pane, so script and layout were split with `flushSync` plus a forced `scrollHeight` read and **paint was not separated**; and every row came out 3-10x below §1's at the same window and DOM size, most likely because §1 timed whole user actions and §8 timed isolated mechanisms — not settled, and the two must not be mixed in one comparison. |

Also found, by reading the refactor that introduced `changeChatTo`, that five call sites still fire their own `$ReloadGUIPointer += 1` one line later — a leftover, not a safety margin — and warned that a fix confined to the helper would not reach the primary click path. That warning is what later killed the first design. Honest limits: two UNCERTAINs left standing with the observation that would settle each. |

| 105 | 2026-09-23 | Gate 1 round 1 on Report 19 §9 rev 1 (defer the bump) | `adversarial-reviewer`, fresh | ~91k, 32 tool uses | n/a (gate) | **[REJECT]**, two design BLOCKERs, and it supplied the design that shipped. **B1:** deferring the bump is defeated by the caller's own synchronous bump one line later, so the expensive fan-out over the outgoing window would still happen and the deferred bump would merely add a second one — the plan would have delivered nothing on its main path. It also identified the specific error: the Orchestrator's "the second bump is free" measurement was real but did not transfer to a design that defers the *first* bump. **B2:** the staleness risk was understated — the script cache key carries no chat identity, so a freshly mounted instance can read a stale entry from the old chat at the same index, not just a reused one. **Its alternative** — `flushSync()` between the write and the bump — dissolved both at no extra size. |

| 106 | 2026-09-23 | Gate 1 round 2 on rev 2 (the `flushSync` design) | `adversarial-reviewer`, fresh | ~131k, 65 tool uses | n/a (gate) | **[APPROVE-WITH-FINDINGS]**, earned by execution rather than argument: it built a Svelte tree mirroring the imperative mount loop and the `onMount` subscription and ran it against this repo's own Svelte 5.55.1 to prove a **freshly mounted** instance subscribes *inside* `flushSync()` and so still receives the corrective bump. That was the one thing that could have made the change worse than the status quo. **MAJOR:** rev 2's "no call site needs editing" was wrong — the two drag-reorder handlers call `changeChatTo` *before* assigning the reordered array, so the new flush would render the wrong chat; and a naive statement swap is also wrong, because the chat *object* must be captured, not the index. A hazard the change itself introduced, missed by both the Orchestrator and round 1. |

| 107 | 2026-09-23 | Implement §9 rev 3: the flush, the reorder seam, and the post-implementation measurement | `test-warrior` (three dispatches), `sonnet-coder` (three dispatches), Orchestrator live measurement | ~234k / ~147k, 72 + 47 tool uses | No | Red-first throughout. The ordering test runs a real `$effect` on `chatPage` and asserts it fired **before** the bump, which only a genuine synchronous flush between the two can satisfy — a spy on "was `flushSync` called" would have been satisfied by calling it in the wrong place. **The test author proved the hard part instead of asserting it:** an end-state assertion provably cannot catch the switch-then-assign bug, since both orders end identically, so it built a mid-flush `$effect` observer and verified by mutation that the observer is what kills it. Also surfaced a durable Svelte-5 testing fact: reassigning an array into `$state` re-proxies it, so elements and arrays must be compared by id, not reference. Measured live against the real build: a chat-list click at 600 mounted went **104.4 ms to 66.2 ms**, non-overlapping ranges. Two process notes worth keeping: an implementer hit a stale `@ts-expect-error` that broke `pnpm check` once the helper existed and **reported it rather than editing the test file it was told not to touch**; and one agent briefly created a scratch file inside the repo to resolve module aliases, then deleted it — confirmed gone. |

Every finding was in a comment, a test or report prose. **MAJOR (tests):** two of its six mutants **survived the full 784-test suite** — reverting the reorder handlers to switch-then-assign, and computing the target against the new array. The suite pinned the function and pinned the helper in isolation but pinned nothing about their wiring at the only two sites that needed it. **MAJOR (comment):** the shipped comment prescribed the *opposite* of the correct rule, telling callers to move dependent writes before the call; the reviewer traced the concrete failure (the branch fold silently never happens) and the Orchestrator verified it in source. Realm upload identity, the `kei` SD provider (it writes `db.account` from `fallbackRisuToken`), the login iframes, `loadRisuAccountData` and the Drive OAuth tokens in `db.account.data` need only the token. Realm browse/download needs no account but relies on `server.cjs`'s `/hub-proxy`, which also relays account-sync traffic for self-hosted Node users. `checkAccountSync()` installs AccountStorage at runtime, so deleting the `Init()` branch alone is not enough. This is inherited from upstream, and no test covers it. A fuzz run of msgpackr 1.10.1 with risuSave's config threw on 20,000 of 20,000 random inputs at 1 KB to 1 MB, but at 16 KB or less it occasionally returned a string or object. The practical risk is negligible; Report 25 invariant 1 closes it, and MC-081 was widened to "any marker entry". (2) `SavePopupIcon.svelte` (always mounted) imports `AccountWarning` and branches on `'account-conflict'`. Report 25 missed it, and so does invariant 4's `\.account\b` grep. (3) No mechanism will handle a stale `accountst` profile once the account UI is gone. `unMigrationAccount` always writes to the plain `risuai` LocalForage, even on Node and OPFS, which is a pre-existing upstream bug. (4) `plugins.md` promises that `pluginStorage` syncs between devices. That is true only through the account cold-storage path being removed; afterwards only `.bin` and Drive carry it. (5) The backup asset loops can throw for account-sync users on hub-only keys (lightning/charx dedup) or on the fork-local 303 `AccountSyncCacheMismatchError`. The global handler alerts; no fork users are affected, and the removal deletes it. (6) Realm anonymous upload is a deliberate upstream feature (f24989c9, 2024-05-31), unchanged since, and HaejeokRisuai made the same cut without reverting it. This is INFERRED, not run. (7) Test files referencing account symbols: 18 tracked, plus 5 W0 files = 23. 1 is wholly account behaviour, 6 are mixed, 16 are incidental. |

(4) `characterURLImport`, and so `getRealmInfo`, runs before `loadedStore.set(true)`; the `?realm=` prompt needs a queued pending path drained after load. (5) Only the `?realm=` prompt was specified to re-post; the click prompts must too, or the integrity-sample toast (row 203, S1) can count as a decline. (6) The self-re-posting stale notice and the `?realm=` prompt can never be live together, but only by position; make it a stated invariant. (7) `bootstrap.staleAccountProfile` and `bootstrap.tauriStaleAccountPin` set `tos4` and narrate the boot `alertTOS`; six suites' `stores.svelte` mocks lack `alertStore`. **Out-of-plan finding:** `Chat.svelte`'s copy button fetches any http(s) URL in rendered content, including `sv.risuai.xyz`, from a click. It is not a Realm or Drive feature, and section 3.5 is silent on it; this is a question for the maintainer. **Orchestrator verification:** re-read both `hubStatus` mappings, `checkDriverInit`'s catch-only strip, `getRealmInfo`'s `pushState`, and `RealmMain`'s top-level `getHub()`. |

It re-derived svelte 5.55.1's `writable` queue: a `set()` inside a subscriber is queued, not recursive. So one self-re-posting alert is safe, but two that re-post against each other would grow the queue forever. R6 holds structurally, not only by position: I6 returns before `checkDriverInit`, `characterURLImport`, `loadPlugins` and `loadedStore.set(true)`, and its OK does a real reload, so no plugin or 28C prompt can run in that page life. The `'tos'` block has no backdrop dismissal; Escape only toasts, and Enter never answers `'tos'`. So a pending prompt always has its own two exits. All of T-C1 to T-C15 can be built on the seams. One placeholder test id covers both `RealmMain` layouts, because the status area sits outside the mobile/desktop split. It ran the two R7 boot test files: 31 of 31 pass. **MAJOR (substantive):** the seam contract did not say that "already accepted" must use `isUpstreamAccepted()`. Once a view subscribes, `get(upstreamAccepted)` returns the cached value (svelte `get()` subscribes and unsubscribes). So a tab would miss acceptance given elsewhere and ask again, against I11, and a T-C1 with no live subscription would not catch it. Fixed: section 11.6 R8, the seam contract's S1, and a new T-C1b that holds a subscription open. **MINOR:** section 3.3's `bootstrap.ts` row did not name R4's post-load drain. Fixed. **NIT:** the `Chat.svelte` copy-button item is soundly reasoned but not yet filed. It waits on the maintainer. **Orchestrator verification:** the `get()` behaviour is svelte's documented store contract, and the fix follows it. |

| 216 | 2026-09-26 | Escalation: CHORE-33 28C's placeholder-control load logic, after three rounds of leaks in the same piece of logic (dossier `scratchpad/28c-escalation/dossier.md`) | `senior-advisor` (Fable 5.1) | ~110k tokens, 28 tool uses | Yes (escalation) | **Redirected: remove the second load trigger rather than guard it.** Diagnosis: the `upstreamAccepted` store could stay `true` while storage held no acceptance, because nothing published a fresh `false` back to it — every fix after round 1 was trying to make the placeholder's control *predict* whether the (silently stale) store would still fire the store-driven effect, and every prediction had a hole (the store can change between the click and the answer; the control can be activated twice). Adopted fix: **Invariant A** (the store never disagrees with the latest fresh read of storage, in either direction; once `getRisuHub`/`getRealmInfo` returns `'consent'`, the store reads `false`) and **Invariant B** (in each view, only the store-driven effect loads in response to acceptance; the control only asks — restoring section 3.3's original ownership statement). The `'consent'` `hubStatus` round 1 introduced is removed. A fresh Gate 2 review runs on the new design; the three-round rejection counter restarts for it (Report 28 section 11.7 rev 3.5). **Judged wrong by this escalation:** round 2's "the consent-`hubStatus`-plus-placeholder design is sound" conclusion. |

| 242 | 2026-09-27 | CHORE-42 scoping (MC-093): what another open tab does after a local restore; detection; platforms; where a guard must sit; other restore-like flows | `investigator` (Sonnet 5) | ~206k, 37 tool uses | no | **Resolved without escalation.** A dirty B overwrites A's restore unconditionally: the restore never broadcasts, and `dbWriteLock` is per page. An idle, clean B does not write. The Node server already 409s B's stale database write (assets and cold storage unguarded). Tauri is single-instance. The exclusive storage lock detects other tabs, and new tabs wait at boot while it is held. It returns `null` both for "another tab" and for "no Web Locks". `acquireExclusiveStorageMigrationLock()` takes `dbWriteLock` first and `AsyncMutex` is not reentrant, so reusing it beside item E's acquire deadlocks (the Orchestrator confirmed this in source). Assets and cold storage are written before the database, so the guard must precede the asset loop. `LoadLocalBackup()`'s async handler has no error path. There is no separate partial-load path, and `loadInternalBackup()` goes through the ordinary save loop. The Orchestrator added: Web Locks are secure-context only, and the app supports plain-HTTP LAN origins (`assetIntegrity.ts`'s `uuidv4()` fallback), so the plan warns instead of refusing when locks are unavailable. Feeds Report 32. |

| 257 | 2026-09-27 | W1a cost measurement on a production build with CPU throttling (Report 24 O-5): the character clone, proxy against plain reads, resolution, and the memo | `perf-analyzer` | ~159k, 73 tool uses | n/a (measurement) | **Measured, in the scratchpad; repo untouched.** Setup: a production Vite build of Svelte 5.56.8; the real `safeStructuredClone` (a `$state` proxy always takes its `rfdc` fallback); `resolveOrigin` re-implemented line for line; headless Chrome 154 over CDP at 1x/4x/6x; i9-13900KF. The clone of a character with 10×100 / 50×500 / 200×500 chats×messages costs 1.8 / 45 / 178 ms at 1x and 14 / 321 / 1271 ms at 6x. Resolution over 500 / 1000 characters × 20 chats costs 64 / 130 µs at 1x and 470 / 950 µs at 6x, whatever the target's position. So one clone equals 14 to 2760 resolutions, and the ratio held within 5% across rates. A `getVar`-style read costs ~0.9 ms at 6x without a memo and ~1.2 µs with one (600-770x). Proxy reads cost 270-300x plain reads. A first run with a 15 ms timing floor gave an impossible inversion (1000 characters cheaper than 500) and was discarded; the Orchestrator checked the authoritative results file against the report. Report 33 §6. |

| 361 | 2026-09-29 | W2c-a: what does the send's prompt pass cost without the script cache? | `perf-analyzer` (scratchpad `w2c/perf/`) | ~120k | n/a | **Measured** (i9-13900KF, node, dev build, `$state` unproxied; best-case hardware). 998 messages of ~400 characters, 8 `editprocess` scripts: the script part of the pass costs ~10-13 ms with the cache warm and ~60 ms with it reset, so bypassing costs ~50 ms per 1,000 messages (~6.3 us per script execution); ~1/3 of real tiktoken time (tokenizer caching off, the default), ~10x it with tokenizer caching on. Plain replaces cost the same as walk-back scripts: the cost is the re-parse after each replace and the regex work. **Surprise:** the 1,000-entry FIFO cache already misses every lookup on a repeat send at 999+ messages (N+2 entries per send; `get` does not refresh), so long chats pay the miss cost at HEAD and upstream; UI entries share the map, so the cliff comes earlier in practice. Under the advisor's rule (30-300 ms band) this selects the purity gate. |

There is no polyfill, no auth-free route for `/api/read`/`/api/write`, and no fallback adapter. Traced cause: upstream `61996dd2` (2026-03-03, first tag `v2026.3.330`) replaced the password-hash auth with client-signed JWTs. From source, plain-HTTP Node should have worked on upstream up to `v2026.2.291`. That was traced, not built or run, and some users may reach a secure context by another route. Localhost booted to the password prompt (observed; first run and save not run). HTTPS was not run. A static build over plain HTTP boots and saves to IndexedDB, but has no Web Locks, OPFS or `crypto.randomUUID`. There, `makeColdData`'s unguarded `crypto.randomUUID()` would throw at boot when the flag is on and a character is idle for 10 days (inferred, not run; the same code is upstream's). For D1: every Node deployment that boots is a secure context with Web Locks. The boot pass must be gated on capabilities (locks and OPFS or the Node server), must use `uuid` v4 for unit ids, and on a host without OPFS must not report units as missing. |

| 500 | 2026-10-01 | Why the manual clean-up's first run refused on a snapshot (row 499): an empty `pluginStorage` block in `database.bin` and `dbbackup-*.bin` | `investigator` | ~145.8k tokens (42 tool uses) | no | **Mechanism found; the clean-up fails safe, so nothing was deleted.** The encoder writes the `pluginStorage` block as `JSON.stringify(data.pluginCustomStorage)` and nothing defaults `pluginCustomStorage`, so a profile that never used plugin storage gets an empty block (0 bytes uncompressed; gzip of empty when compressed). The decoder's `PLUGIN_STORAGE` case runs `JSON.parse("")`: the normal decode logs it and skips it (the field stays absent), and the strict decode rejects the whole file. Both were run against the real encoder and decoder in scratch probes. Only `pluginStorage` can be legitimately empty; every other block has a default or always has content. Upstream writes the same empty block: `upstream/main` `risuSave.ts` has the same two `JSON.stringify(data.pluginCustomStorage)` lines and upstream `database.svelte.ts` has no default (the Orchestrator re-checked with `git show upstream/main:...`), so users arriving from upstream bring such main files and snapshots. The only non-test strict caller is `manualCleanup.ts` (`decodeStrictly`; the Orchestrator re-ran the grep), so the defect makes the manual clean-up always refuse for such a profile. The v2 data checksum covers the empty payload, so accepting an empty block cannot hide a damaged one in fork-written files. Also from the packet (read, not run): fork-written `database.bin` files and snapshots start `RISUSAVE\x01` and do not load in upstream's decoder at all; the exported backup uses the legacy encoder. **Decision (Orchestrator):** fix the decoder only. An empty `pluginStorage` block decodes as the field being absent, in strict and normal reads alike; every other strict rejection is unchanged; the encoder is unchanged. The plan gate is skipped under the carve-out (one `case`, obvious shape); the post-implementation gate goes to `opus-reviewer`. At the time of this row the fix was in progress and not implemented. No escalation was needed, so escalation changed no conclusion. |

Network: `GET /api/test_auth` 200, `POST /api/crypto` 200, `POST /api/set_password` 200, `GET /api/read` 400 with `{"error":"Unknown Public Key"}`. After a reload the app asks "Input your password..." and the same password boots normally. Mechanism (source read by the Orchestrator; not traced beyond the read): `NodeStorage.checkAuth` (`src/ts/storage/nodeStorage.ts`) answers the `unset` state by POSTing the password to `/api/set_password` and returning, without calling `/api/login` and without setting `authChecked`; only `/api/login` adds the public key's hash to `knownPublicKeysHashes`, and the JWT check on `/api/read` answers 400 for a key not in that list. Origin: the client's set-password path is upstream `74f76255` (2023-05-28) and the public-key list is upstream `61996dd2` (2026-03-03), so upstream has had the same first-run failure since 2026-03-03 (inferred from source; upstream not run). Not fixed and not scheduled. |

Archived characters and chats round-trip. Device-local stores (inlay media, HypaMemory caches, plugin permissions) are in neither exporter (TRACED from source, not run). The fork-added database fields are ignored by upstream without harm. None of it was caused by CHORE-55. CHORE-19: the message body's colour comes from `.chattext p { color: var(--FontColorStandard) }` and the bold, italic and quote `--FontColor*` variables, light on dark schemes; mobilechat's editor textarea uses `text-textcolor`; cardboard's editor is already `text-black`, so the cardboard problem is the rendered body only; why user messages looked readable was not explained by code (UNCERTAIN). Correction: the Roadmap's line numbers for CHORE-56 are stale (`initMobileGesture` is now at `hotkey.ts:514-563`). ## Reading of the log

### Reading at row 385 (2026-09-29)

Every figure below is counted from the rows of this file by the script at the end of this
section, unless it says "by hand". **The reading is as of row 385:** the script ignores later
rows, and the figures stay as they are when rows are added. Where the rows are inconsistent or silent, the text says so
rather than estimating. This is a reading of a log kept by the people running the campaign, not a
controlled comparison.

**What the log is.** 381 rows. Numbers run 1 to 385; 371-374 were reserved and never used. Every
row has the seven columns of the schema.

**Rows by tier.** A row can name several agents, so the first count adds up to more than 381.
- **Named in the tier column:**
  - reviewers 202 (`opus-reviewer` 154, `adversarial-reviewer` 48);
  - `investigator` 57;
  - `perf-analyzer` 12;
  - `senior-advisor` 12;
  - `code-searcher` 10;
  - `deep-investigator` 6;
  - other 111 rows, which name (these overlap) `test-warrior` 53, `sonnet-coder` 43, Orchestrator-run
    checks 39, `doc-verifier` 5, `translator` 5, `doc-writer` 2 and the pre-split
    `opus-investigator` 1.
- **First-named agent only, each row once** (adds to 381): reviewers 193, other 104,
  `investigator` 57, `perf-analyzer` 12, `senior-advisor` 12, `code-searcher` 2,
  `deep-investigator` 1.
- `code-searcher` is named in 10 rows but is the first-named agent in 2: it is mostly a survey
  inside an `investigator` row.

**Escalations to `deep-investigator`: 2 on the AGENTS.md 1.3 triggers (rows 14 and 192).**
- 54 `investigator` rows do not name `deep-investigator`: 51 say "No", 2 say "Read-only" and 1 says
  "Requested" (row 253). The other three (173, 174, 192) are workflow rows: 173 and 174
  use `deep-investigator` as a built-in verify stage; 192 dispatched it on two contradictions the
  critic found.
- The one requested escalation (row 253, a design fork, not a fact dispute) was routed to
  `senior-advisor` (row 255) instead. That escalation changed the design: W-2 was replaced by W-2′.
- `deep-investigator` appears in 6 rows: 14, 173, 174, 176, 177, 192.
  - **Row 14** escalated from Reports 01 and 01-deepdive (static reports, not an `investigator`
    row) at the maintainer's request, and **it changed the conclusion**.
  - **Row 192** dispatched it on two contradictions that its critic found, which is a 1.3 trigger.
    It **confirmed** Invariant 6.
  - **Rows 173 and 174** are the by-design verify stages. They record no change.
  - **Rows 176 and 177** record that the verify stage was not triggered.
- So for "`investigator` questions that escalated to `deep-investigator`": 1 (row 192), and it did
  not change the conclusion. Counting row 14, which did not start from an `investigator` row, there
  are 2 trigger-based uses of the tier, and 1 of the 2 changed the conclusion. The 2 by-design
  verifications record no change.
- **The "Escalated?" column cannot be summed.** 28 cells start with "Yes". By hand, 15 of those
  mean "ran it" (live checks, a measurement probe, a vitest run), 9 are escalations (`deep-investigator`
  row 14 and `senior-advisor` rows 97, 133, 161, 168, 175, 216, 323, 338), 1 points to another row
  (215) and 3 are workflow rows (173 and 174 verify stages; 192 contradiction checks). 13 more cells start with other text such as "Probes in
  scratch" or "Ran `pnpm test`".

**`senior-advisor`: 12 rows** (71, 97, 133, 161, 168, 175, 216, 255, 274, 323, 338, 360). By hand,
from the rows' own questions:
- **The three-[REJECT] rule or its "stuck in a loop" form: 8.** Row 97 (after five rejected
  designs), 133 (three consecutive Gate 2 rejections), 168, 274, 323, 338 and 360 (three Gate 1
  rejections each), and 216 (three rounds of leaks in one piece of logic; the rows show two
  [REJECT] verdicts and an interrupted third round).
- **Other: 4.** Row 71 (a strategy recommendation for CHORE-17, not an escalation), 161 (the writer
  rework direction, an `MC-073` escalation the maintainer approved), 175 (the scope, migration
  guarantee and timing of the RisuAccount removal, escalation authorised by the maintainer) and 255
  (whether W1 could keep W-2, raised by the `investigator` in row 253).
- **Changed the direction, in the rows' own words:** rows 97, 133, 216, 255, 323 and 338 (6). The
  other six give a root cause or a direction and do not say whether it overturned the plan.
- **Against the architecture:** 5 of the 8 rule-triggered escalations (133, 274, 323, 338, 360) were
  followed by further [REJECT] rounds on the same gate: 133 by 3 rounds and a final-check row (141),
  274 by 3, 323 by 3, 338 by 1 and 360 by 1. The escalation fixed
  direction, not the gate. The reviewers' own descriptions of those rounds: wording only (133),
  rules added to handle text typed during a wait, until the maintainer's lock removed the case (274;
  Report 22), definitions and scenarios (323; Report 36), bounded plan defects (338), and the
  `MC-111`/`MC-112` work that the maintainer then split out (360).

**Gate outcomes** (script section 3; the gates are listed by hand in the script, and a check that
every reject row belongs to a listed gate found none left over; row 76, a commit-message check, is
left out). Only gates with at least one
[REJECT] row are listed, so this gives no rate: gates that passed first time are not counted.
- **Plan gates with at least one [REJECT]: 24. With three or more: 7.** Report 19's chat window
  (5), Report 22's composer (6), Report 24's W0 (3), Report 28's CHORE-33 (3), Report 36's W2b-core
  (6), Report 38's W2b-previews (4) and Report 40's W2c-a (4).
- **Code gates with at least one [REJECT]: 28. With three or more: 2.** CHORE-01 Stage 1 (3) and
  durable drafts (7).
- Counted as verdict tokens, consecutive or not. Caveats: rows before the 2026-09-26 taxonomy use
  "REJECT" for wording-only findings too (CHORE-01 Stage 1's third and durable drafts' rounds 4-6
  and final check are wording only). CHORE-33's Gate 1 count includes a wording-only fix-up review
  (row 196), so its substantive count is 2. CHORE-33 28C's Gate 2 has two [REJECT] verdicts and an
  interrupted third round, so it is not in the count; it was escalated (row 216).

**Token cost by tier.** From single-agent, non-workflow rows whose token cell starts with a figure.
Cells that say "more", "cumulative" or "combined" are left out and reported apart. 58 rows give no
figure (51 of them "n/a" or "not given"). Medians are of what the rows state, which is not one consistent quantity.
- `investigator`: n=38, 55.5k to 343k, median 152k (rows 110 and 355).
- `adversarial-reviewer`: n=38, 45k to 276k, median 132k (rows 351 and 376).
- `opus-reviewer`: n=96, 12k to 321k, median 141k (rows 347 and 261).
- `perf-analyzer`: n=8, 45k to 426k, median 156k (rows 314 and 80).
- `senior-advisor`: n=10, 94k to 213k, median 114k (rows 338 and 175).
- `sonnet-coder`: n=15, 20k to 444k, median 160k. `test-warrior`: n=19, 90k to 408k, median 195k.
- One data point each: `deep-investigator` 161k (row 14), `code-searcher` 73k (row 190).
- Reused reviewers report the increment ("~Nk more"): 27 rows, 10k to 155k, median 30k.
- Workflow rows (18) give totals from 124k (row 197) to 2.18M (row 192).
- The `deep-investigator` tier has one row, so this log cannot compare its cost with the
  `investigator` tier's.

**Failures traced to bad investigation.** No row records a wrong investigation reaching a commit.
Rows do record investigator-tier errors caught before use (found by a keyword search of the
`investigator`, `code-searcher` and workflow rows, then read; the search can miss some):
- row 19 (one wrong claim), 21 (two errors, one turning a "neutralised" finding into a live
  asset-deletion hypothesis), 157 (missed a second loss, found by the Orchestrator reading
  `sendMain`), 178 (a wrong claim about a test), 190 (`code-searcher`: four headline counts overcounted its own listings; separately, a Git Bash
  path rewrite made `/kei` appear to have no matches, missed by the agent and by the Orchestrator's
  re-run, and corrected by row 192), 192 (two packet errors caught) and 202 (a
  lens's rating overstated);
- **row 185 is the serious one:** the packet called `sendMain` nonexistent and "refuted" a citation
  in `MC-074` that was right. That wrong "correction" was recorded, and Gate 1 round 1 of the plan
  caught it (row 186, MAJOR-1);
- row 1 (the pre-split Opus investigation) understated two things.
- Every one was caught by the Orchestrator's verification or a gate reviewer, none by
  `deep-investigator`.

**What this argues against the current architecture.**
- The Opus investigation tier is close to unused: two trigger-based uses in 381 rows. The log cannot
  say whether that is because mid-tier packets are good enough or because the escalation bar is too
  high, since the checking was done by the Orchestrator and by gate reviewers. The
  "escalations that changed the conclusion" metric stands at 1 of 2 (row 14 changed it; row 192
  confirmed).
- The cost is in review, not investigation: reviewers are named in 202 of 381 rows, and 154 of those
  name `opus-reviewer`.
- Nine gates took three or more [REJECT] rounds, and 5 of 8 rule-triggered `senior-advisor`
  escalations did not end the gate's rejections.
- 8 rows record errors: 7 `investigator` or workflow rows (of 57) and 1 `code-searcher` row (of 10
  that name it). All were caught by manual verification, so the Orchestrator's standing
  verification duty (AGENTS.md 1.3) is load-bearing: row 185 shows what passes without it.
- Rows are not comparable: the "Escalated?" column is used for several meanings, reused reviewers
  log increments, 58 rows give no token figure, and older gate rows use a different verdict vocabulary.
  The log cannot produce a clean escalation rate.

**What it supports.** The three-[REJECT] rule has been applied: every gate that reached three
substantive [REJECT] rounds in a row since row 165 was escalated before a fourth (rows 168, 274,
323, 338, 360), at 94k to 129k tokens each. The rows say six of the 12 `senior-advisor` calls changed the
direction.

**The script.** Run as `node ledger-reading.mjs Agents/Investigation-Ledger.md`. The gate lists in
section 3 and the tier grouping are judgements made by hand; the counts follow from them.

```js
// Counts the figures in "Reading at row 385" from Agents/Investigation-Ledger.md.
// Run: node ledger-reading.mjs <path to Investigation-Ledger.md>
import fs from 'node:fs'
const text = fs.readFileSync(process.argv[2], 'utf8').replace(/\r\n/g, '\n')
const rows = text.split('\n').filter(l => /^\| \d+ \| /.test(l)).map(l => {
  const c = l.split(' | ')   // every log row has exactly 7 cells
  return { n: +c[0].slice(2), tier: c[3], tok: c[4], esc: c[5], out: c[6].replace(/\*\*/g, '') }
}).filter(r => r.n <= 385)   // the reading is "as of row 385"; later rows do not change its figures
const byN = new Map(rows.map(r => [r.n, r]))
const AG = /(deep-investigator|opus-investigator|investigator|code-searcher|perf-analyzer|adversarial-reviewer|opus-reviewer|senior-advisor|sonnet-coder|test-warrior|doc-writer|doc-verifier|translator|code-reader|Orchestrator)/g
const agents = s => [...new Set([...s.matchAll(AG)].map(m => m[1]))]
const tierOf = a => ({ 'adversarial-reviewer': 'reviewers', 'opus-reviewer': 'reviewers', investigator: 'investigator',
  'deep-investigator': 'deep-investigator', 'code-searcher': 'code-searcher', 'perf-analyzer': 'perf-analyzer',
  'senior-advisor': 'senior-advisor' }[a] || 'other')

// 1. Rows by tier. A row that names several agents counts once in each tier it names.
const named = {}, primary = {}
for (const r of rows) {
  const ag = agents(r.tier)
  for (const t of new Set(ag.map(tierOf))) named[t] = (named[t] || 0) + 1
  const p = tierOf(ag[0] || 'Orchestrator'); primary[p] = (primary[p] || 0) + 1
}
console.log('rows', rows.length, '| named in tier:', JSON.stringify(named), '| first-named only:', JSON.stringify(primary))

// 2. Token ranges: single-agent, non-workflow rows whose token cell starts with "~N k|M".
//    Cells saying "more", "cumulative" or "combined" are reused-reviewer increments; reported separately.
const kTok = s => { const m = s.match(/^~?\s*([\d.,]+)\s*([kM])\b/); return m ? parseFloat(m[1].replace(/,/g, '')) * (m[2] === 'M' ? 1000 : 1) : null }
const groups = {}, incr = []
for (const r of rows) {
  const ag = agents(r.tier), k = kTok(r.tok)
  if (/Workflow/i.test(r.tier) || ag.length !== 1 || k == null || /\+/.test(r.tok.split(',')[0])) continue
  if (/more|cumulative|combined|first run|follow-up/i.test(r.tok)) { if (/more/.test(r.tok)) incr.push(k); continue }
  ;(groups[ag[0]] ||= []).push([r.n, k])
}
const med = a => { const s = [...a].sort((x, y) => x - y), m = s.length >> 1; return s.length % 2 ? s[m] : (s[m - 1] + s[m]) / 2 }
for (const [a, v] of Object.entries(groups)) {
  const ks = v.map(x => x[1]); const lo = v.find(x => x[1] === Math.min(...ks)), hi = v.find(x => x[1] === Math.max(...ks))
  console.log(`tokens ${a}: n=${v.length} min ${lo[1]}k (row ${lo[0]}) median ${med(ks)}k max ${hi[1]}k (row ${hi[0]})`)
}
const noFigure = rows.filter(r => !/[\d.,]+\s*[kM]\b/.test(r.tok))
console.log(`rows with no token figure: ${noFigure.length} (${noFigure.filter(r => /^(n\/a|not given)/i.test(r.tok)).length} of them "n/a" or "not given")`)
console.log(`reused-reviewer increments ("~Nk more"): n=${incr.length} min ${Math.min(...incr)}k median ${med(incr)}k max ${Math.max(...incr)}k`)

// 3. Gate rounds. Each gate is listed by hand (its rows, in order); a row counts as a reject when its
//    outcome text names REJECT in its first 90 characters. Row 158 (rounds 1 and 2 in one row) counts twice.
//    Row 76 is a commit-message check, not a gate, and is left out.
const G = {
  plan: {
    'Report 10 Stage B draft copy': [4, 6], 'Report 17 CHORE-01': [63], 'Report 19 chat window': [92, 93, 94, 95, 96, 98],
    'Report 19 s9 defer the bump': [105, 106], 'Home stage 2': [111, 112], 'Home stage 3': [116, 117],
    'Report 22 composer': [158, 273, 275, 276, 277, 280], 'Report 24 W0': [165, 166, 167, 170],
    'Report 26 CHORE-28': [179, 180], 'Report 27 CHORE-34': [186, 187], 'Report 28 CHORE-33': [193, 194, 195, 196, 197],
    'Report 30 CHORE-39': [226, 227], 'Report 31 removal': [233, 234, 235, 236], 'Report 32 CHORE-42': [243, 246, 247],
    'Report 33 W1a': [258, 259, 260], 'Report 34 W1b': [266, 267], 'Report 22 S2': [292, 293],
    'Report 35 W2a': [307, 308, 311], 'Report 36 W2b-core': [320, 321, 322, 324, 325, 326, 327],
    'Report 38 W2b-previews': [335, 336, 337, 339, 340], 'Report 39 Escape 1': [349, 350, 351],
    'Report 40 W2c-a': [357, 358, 359, 362, 363], 'Report 41 Escape 2': [376, 377], 'Report 42 W2c-b': [384, 385],
  },
  code: {
    'Stage B partition': [11], 'CHORE-07 7a': [29, 33, 34], 'CHORE-07 7b': [40], 'CHORE-07 7c-1': [42], 'AV-3': [50],
    'AV-4': [57, 58], 'CHORE-01 Stage 1': [64, 67, 68], 'CHORE-01 Stage 2': [73, 74, 75], 'CHORE-17': [83, 84, 85],
    'Home stage 2': [113], 'Home stage 3': [118, 119], 'Durable drafts': [128, 130, 132, 136, 137, 139, 140, 141],
    'W0': [171, 172, 176, 177], 'CHORE-28': [181, 182, 183, 184], 'CHORE-34': [188, 189], 'CHORE-33 28A': [198, 199],
    'CHORE-33 28B': [203, 204], 'CHORE-33 28C': [213, 214, 215, 220, 222], 'CHORE-39': [229, 230],
    'Removal stage': [237, 238, 239], 'CHORE-42': [249, 250], 'W1a': [261, 262], 'W1b': [268, 269],
    'Composer S1': [284, 286, 288], 'Composer S2': [297, 298], 'W2a': [315, 316], 'W2b-core': [330, 331],
    'W2c-a': [367, 368],
  },
}
const isReject = r => /REJECT/.test(r.out.slice(0, 90)) && !/^Interrupted/.test(r.out)
const override = { 158: 2, 181: 1 }   // 181: the evidence lens rejected while the data-safety lens approved
const covered = new Set([76])
for (const kind of ['plan', 'code']) {
  const list = Object.entries(G[kind]).map(([name, ns]) => {
    ns.forEach(n => covered.add(n))
    const rej = ns.reduce((a, n) => a + (override[n] ?? (isReject(byN.get(n)) ? 1 : 0)), 0)
    return { name, rej, rounds: ns.length }
  })
  const three = list.filter(g => g.rej >= 3)
  console.log(`${kind} gates with at least one reject: ${list.filter(g => g.rej > 0).length}; with 3 or more: ${three.length}`)
  three.forEach(g => console.log(`   ${g.name}: ${g.rej} rejects in ${g.rounds} rows`))
}
const uncovered = rows.filter(r => isReject(r) && !covered.has(r.n)).map(r => r.n)
console.log('reject rows not in any listed gate:', uncovered.join(',') || 'none')

// 4. Escalations named in the rows
const sa = rows.filter(r => /senior-advisor/.test(r.tier)).map(r => r.n)
console.log('senior-advisor rows:', sa.join(','))
const deep = rows.filter(r => /deep-investigator/.test(r.tier)).map(r => r.n)
console.log('deep-investigator rows:', deep.join(','))

// 5. The "Escalated?" column: investigator-tier rows by what the cell says, and rows whose cell starts "Yes"
const invRows = rows.filter(r => /`investigator`/.test(r.tier) && !/deep-investigator/.test(r.tier))
const cell = r => /^\W*no\b/i.test(r.esc) ? 'No' : /^n\/a/i.test(r.esc) ? 'n/a' : r.esc.replace(/\*\*/g, '').slice(0, 40)
const cls = {}; invRows.forEach(r => (cls[cell(r)] ||= []).push(r.n))
console.log('investigator rows (plain, no workflow):', invRows.length, JSON.stringify(Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(cls).map(([k, v]) => [k, v.length]))))
console.log('rows whose Escalated? cell starts with Yes:', rows.filter(r => /^\W*yes/i.test(r.esc)).length)
```

### Early observations (n=1)

Written when the log had one row. Still valid. Its closing advice not to conclude on the escalation
rate is superseded by the reading above, which counts the post-split rows.

Run 1 is the only data point and it predates the split, so it cannot settle anything. Three
observations that motivated the split, recorded while they are fresh:

1. **The valuable output was concentrated, and thinner than it first appeared.** Of the two
   findings that looked like they justified the Opus tier, only one survived: the
   `getModules()` load-bearing cache accident was real and decision-changing, while the
   DEV-inflation doubt was tested and disproven (1.18x, not a multiple). Most of the 43 tool
   calls were mechanical archaeology producing no reasoning of either kind. One genuine
   load-bearing find per ~100k tokens is the bar the mid-tier now has to clear — and it is a
   lower bar than the raw report made it look.
2. **Orchestrator verification caught real gaps in an Opus packet.** Both understatements
   above came from an *Opus* investigation, not a cheap one. This is the argument for the
   standing verification duty in 1.3 being tier-independent: the failure mode is
   compression, not model capability. It also means a mid-tier packet should not be held to
   a standard the Opus tier did not meet.
3. **A flagged doubt is not a finding until it is tested.** The Opus packet correctly
   identified a real DEV-only code path and correctly labelled its magnitude UNCERTAIN. The
   Orchestrator then spent a `perf-analyzer` dispatch settling it, and the answer was "no
   material effect." That sequence was correct and should be repeated — but it means an
   investigation's value should be scored on premises *settled*, not premises *raised*.

Do not draw a conclusion about escalation rate until there are enough post-split rows to
have one.
